Metric uniform distribution on analytic curves
Authors: Vitaly Bergelson, Joel Moreira
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We obtain multidimensional metric uniform distribution results involving sequences in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Rk parametrized by analytic curves. Our theorems extend the classical theorems of Weyl and Koksma in a variety of ways. One of our main results implies that for any injective sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→Z the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Big \{(x_1,\dots ,x_k)\in \mathbb {R}^k:\big (a_1(n)x_1,\dots ,a_k(n)x_k\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}\text { is uniformly distributed in }\mathbb {T}^k\Big \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{(x1,⋯,xk)∈Rk:(a1(n)x1,⋯,ak(n)xk)n∈Nis uniformly distributed inTk}has full Lebesgue measure inside any non-degenerate analytic curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \subset \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}γ⊂Rk.

Introduction

Background and description of the main results

It was Borel who showed in [2] that for every natural number b≥ 2b≥2, almost every real number x is normal in base b, meaning that each finite sequence of digits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in \{0,\dots ,b-1\}^n$$\end{document}w∈{0,⋯,b-1}n appears in the b-adic expansion of x with frequency \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/b^n$$\end{document}1/bn. It was eventually revealed that Borel’s theorem is equivalent to a special case (Theorem 1.3) of a basic result in the theory of uniform distribution modulo one established by Weyl in [15]. The goal of our paper is to provide an amplification of the aforementioned result of Weyl which deals with uniform distribution on analytic curves in higher-dimensional tori. Along the way, we trace the historic development of the ideas triggered by Weyl’s seminal paper. We start with introducing the relevant definitions. Throughout the paper, we denote by |X| the cardinality of the (finite) set X and follow the convention that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}=\{1,2,\dots \}$$\end{document}N={1,2,⋯}.

Definition 1.1

(Uniform distribution) Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N. A sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xn)n∈N in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Rk is uniformly distributed modulo 1 if for every intervals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_1,\dots ,I_k\subset [0,1]$$\end{document}I1,⋯,Ik⊂[0,1],1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{N}\Big |\big \{n\in \{1,\dots ,N\}:\{x_n\}\in I_1\times \cdots \times I_k\big \}\Big |=\mu (I_1\times \cdots \times I_k). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞1N|{n∈{1,⋯,N}:{xn}∈I1×⋯×Ik}|=μ(I1×⋯×Ik).where μ μ denotes the k-dimensional Lebesgue measure and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=(y_1,\dots ,y_k)\in \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}y=(y1,⋯,yk)∈Rk we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{y\}$$\end{document}{y} for the vector of fractional parts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\{y_1\},\dots ,\{y_k\})$$\end{document}({y1},⋯,{yk}).

Since the uniform distribution modulo 1 of a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xn)n∈N in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Rk only depends on the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (x_n)$$\end{document}π(xn), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi :\mathbb {R}^k\rightarrow \mathbb {T}^k=\mathbb {R}^k/\mathbb {Z}^k$$\end{document}π:Rk→Tk=Rk/Zk is the natural projection we will often say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xn)n∈N is u.d. in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk instead of uniformly distributed modulo 1.

Along with the definition, in [15] Weyl also introduced (and effectively used) the following criterion for uniform distribution, which still remains a fundamental tool for establishing a wide range of results in the theory of uniform distribution.

Theorem 1.2

(Weyl’s criterion) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xn)n∈N be a sequence in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Rk. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_n)$$\end{document}(xn) is uniformly distributed mod 1 if and only if for every nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathbb {Z}^k$$\end{document}v∈Zk,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne(v\cdot x_n)=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞1N∑n=1Ne(v·xn)=0,where v· xv·x denotes the usual scalar product in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Rk.

One can show that Borel’s theorem is equivalent to the following statement pertaining to uniform distribution modulo one.

Theorem 1.3

For each b≥ 2b≥2, for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R (with respect to Lebesgue measure) the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(b^nx)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(bnx)n∈N is uniformly distributed modulo one.

The equivalence between Borel’s theorem and Theorem 1.3 was first observed by Wall in his thesis [14, Theorem 1]. (The proof can be found also in [7, Chapter 1, Theorem 8.1].) Theorem 1.3 is a rather special case of the following more general result obtained by Weyl in [15].

Theorem 1.4

For any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a:N→R satisfying1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \inf _n\big (a(n+1)-a(n)\big )>0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}infn(a(n+1)-a(n))>0,the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)x\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)x)n∈N is uniformly distributed modulo one for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R.

We stress that the special case in Theorem 1.3 can also be derived from the pointwise ergodic theorem, but the full strength of Theorem 1.4 does not fit in an ergodic theoretic framework. In fact, in [15] Weyl proved a stronger variant of Theorem 1.4 (see Theorem 2.11) that requires only a weaker form of (1.2). In this paper, we consider a different weaker form of (1.2) and call a sequence satisfying it scattered. The precise definition of scattered sequence is postponed until Definition 2.13, but the reader may safely think of it as an averaged form of (1.2) that allows for a wider class of examples; a full discussion on scattered sequences, and their connection to both condition (1.2) and Weyl’s original condition is presented in Sect. 2.4.

In this paper, we are interested in “non-trivial” multidimensional extensions of Weyl’s theorem. We first note that it follows easily from Theorem 1.4 and Weyl’s criterion that given a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a:N→R satisfying (1.2), for almost every point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y)\in \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}(x,y)∈R2 the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)x,a(n)y\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)x,a(n)y)n∈N is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 (cf. the stronger Proposition 4.1). This fact is related to a more general theorem obtained by Philipp in [9], stating that for any sequence a(n) of d× dd×d non-singular matrices with integers entries and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det (a(n)-a(m))\ne 0$$\end{document}det(a(n)-a(m))≠0 for all n≠ mn≠m, for almost every vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=(x_1,\dots ,x_d)\in \mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}x=(x1,⋯,xd)∈Td the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a(n)x)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)x)n∈N is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Td.

The goal of this paper is to address the more subtle question of whether given a (sufficiently regular) curve γ γ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn one can have a Weyl-type theorem that applies to almost every point in γ γ. For instance, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a:N→R satisfies (1.2), is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)x,a(n)x^2\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)x,a(n)x2)n∈N uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R? More generally, assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,p_2\in \mathbb {R}[x]$$\end{document}p1,p2∈R[x] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a,b:N→R; under which conditions (on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b,p_1,p_2$$\end{document}a,b,p1,p2) is the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)p_1(x),b(n)p_2(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)p1(x),b(n)p2(x))n∈N uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R? Observe that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1=p_2$$\end{document}p1=p2 and a=ba=b, then the sequence takes values in the diagonal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{(x,x):x\in \mathbb {T}\}$$\end{document}{(x,x):x∈T} and hence cannot be uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2. The following result gives an answer to some of these questions.

Theorem 1.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\cdots , p_k\in \mathbb {R}[x]$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk∈R[x]. Then, the following are equivalent: (1)The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} is linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.(2)For every scattered sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R, for Lebesgue-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)p_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)p_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)p1(x),⋯,ak(n)pk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} is linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.

For every scattered sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R, for Lebesgue-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)p_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)p_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)p1(x),⋯,ak(n)pk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Theorem 1.5 follows from the stronger Theorem 3.1. It implies, for example, that the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)x,a(n)x^2,\dots ,a(n)x^k\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)x,a(n)x2,⋯,a(n)xk)n∈N is u.d. mod 1 for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R (whenever a is scattered). However, this result does not guarantee that the same conclusion holds for the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)x,a(n)^2x, \dots ,a(n)^kx\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)x,a(n)2x,⋯,a(n)kx)n∈N. The following theorem shows that, rather than assuming that the polynomials are linearly independent, one can instead impose an independence condition on the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_i(n)$$\end{document}ai(n). Given sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R, we say that they are jointly scattered if for every nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=(v_1,\dots ,v_k)\in \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}v=(v1,⋯,vk)∈Rk the linear combination \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1a_1+\cdots +v_ka_k$$\end{document}v1a1+⋯+vkak is scattered.

Theorem 1.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\cdots , p_k\in \mathbb {R}[x]$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk∈R[x] be non-constant polynomials, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R be jointly scattered. Then for Lebesgue-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)p_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)p_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)p1(x),⋯,ak(n)pk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Theorem 1.6 implies, for example, that for any scattered sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a:N→R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)x,a(n)^2x, \dots ,a(n)^kx\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)x,a(n)2x,⋯,a(n)kx)n∈N is u.d. mod 1 for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R. Actually, if a(n) is scattered, then P∘ aP∘a is scattered for a large class of functions P which includes all non-constant polynomials; see Lemma 2.14.

The following theorem, which is proved in Sect. 3, extends the scope of Theorems 1.5 and 1.6 by replacing polynomials with general analytic functions.

Theorem 1.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be non-constant analytic functions, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R be scattered.

Assume that either The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,orThe functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} are linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.Then for a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,or

The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,

or

The functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} are linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.

The functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} are linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.

Remark 1.8

The condition that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i$$\end{document}fi are analytic in Theorem 1.7 cannot be relaxed to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i\in C^\infty $$\end{document}fi∈C∞. Indeed there exist non-constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\infty $$\end{document}C∞ functions such that f(x)=2f(x)=2 for a positive measure set of x. So, if a(n)=na(n)=n, then a(n)f(x) is an integer for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N and every x in a positive measure set and hence cannot be u.d. mod 1 for almost every x. For more discussion on possible relaxations of the analytic condition, see Sect. 2.6.

Here is an equivalent formulation of Theorem 1.7 in geometric terms. An analytic curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \subset \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}γ⊂Rk is the image of a real analytic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}f:R→Rk. It is non-degenerate if it is not contained in a proper hyperplane.

Theorem 1.9

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \subset \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}γ⊂Rk be an analytic curve, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R be scattered. Assume that either the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered or that the curve is non-degenerate. Then for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_1,\dots ,x_k)\in \gamma $$\end{document}(x1,⋯,xk)∈γ, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_1(n)x_1,\dots ,a_k(n)x_k)$$\end{document}(a1(n)x1,⋯,ak(n)xk) is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

We remark that Theorem 1.9 admits a “multiparameter” variant where the curve γ γ is replaced by an analytic manifold in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Rk of any dimension. This variant follows easily from Theorem 1.9 via a Fubini-type argument.

In [6], Koksma proved an extension of Theorem 1.4 that implies that the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^{a(n)})_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xa(n))n∈N is u.d. mod 1 for almost every x>1x>1, provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a:N→R satisfies (1.2). Our next result is a joint generalization of this fact and Theorems 1.5 and 1.6:

Theorem 1.10

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow (1,\infty )$$\end{document}g:R→(1,∞) be a non-constant analytic function, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}b:N→R tend to ∞ ∞ and be scattered. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\cdots , p_k\in \mathbb {R}[x]$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk∈R[x] be non-constant polynomials, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R be scattered. Assume that either The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,orThe polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} are linearly independent.Then, for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (g(x)^{b(n)},a_1(n)p_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)p_k(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g(x)b(n),a1(n)p1(x),⋯,ak(n)pk(x)) is u.d. in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^{k+1}$$\end{document}Tk+1.

The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,or

The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,

or

The polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} are linearly independent.

The polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} are linearly independent.

It seems reasonable to expect that an extension of Theorem 1.10, in a way similar to how Theorem 1.7 extends Theorems 1.5 and 1.6, should hold, where polynomials are replaced by general analytic functions. Unfortunately, our techniques do not seem sufficient to obtain such a result. In this direction, we make the following conjecture (see also Sect. 4.2 for other potential extensions of Theorem 1.10).

Conjecture 1.11

Theorem 1.10 holds when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\dots ,p_k$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk are general non-constant analytic functions.

In support of Conjecture 1.11, we have the following result, corresponding to the case k=1k=1, which can be obtained as a corollary of Theorem 1.10 via a “change of variable” maneuver.

Corollary 1.12

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,g:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f,g:R→R be non-constant analytic functions such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\mathbb {R})\subset (1,\infty )$$\end{document}g(R)⊂(1,∞) and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a,b:N→R be scattered sequences. Then, for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (g(x)^{b(n)},a(n)f(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g(x)b(n),a(n)f(x)) is u.d. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2.

We conclude this section with the formulation of a general question which encompasses all the results formulated above.

Question 1.13

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1,\dots ,g_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow (1,\infty )$$\end{document}g1,⋯,gk:R→(1,∞) be analytic functions, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k,b_1,\dots ,b_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak,b1,⋯,bk:N→R be scattered. Under which conditions on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i,g_i,a_i,b_i$$\end{document}fi,gi,ai,bi is it true that for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Big (g_1(x)^{b_1(n)}\,\ \dots \,\ g_k(x)^{b_k(n)}\,\ a_1(n)f_1(x)\,\ \dots \,\ a_k(n)f_k(x)\Big )_{n=1}^\infty \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(g1(x)b1(n)⋯gk(x)bk(n)a1(n)f1(x)⋯ak(n)fk(x))n=1∞is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^{2k}$$\end{document}T2k?

Structure of the paper

The paper is organized as follows: Sect. 2 is a partly expository section dedicated to an introduction to metric equidistribution theorems following the footsteps of Weyl and Koksma. In particular, we provide modern proofs of Weyl’s theorem and Koksma’s theorem; showcase the role of asymptotic orthogonality and demonstrate how it leads to amplifications of a classical theorem of Raikov; and introduce and explore the notion of scattered sequences, including comparison with Weyl’s growth condition and finally prove Theorem 1.6.

In Sect. 3, we first prove Theorem 3.1, which extends the scope of Theorem 1.5. Its proof combines the arguments used to derive Theorem 1.6 with the following oscillatory integral estimate, due to J. Wright, the proof of which is given in the appendix.

Theorem 1.14

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be analytic functions such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} is linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R be a compact interval.

Then, there exist C,δ >0C,δ>0 such that for every nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda =(\lambda _1,\dots ,\lambda _k)\in \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}λ=(λ1,⋯,λk)∈Rk,1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| \int _Ie\big (\lambda _1f_1(x)+\cdots +\lambda _kf_k(x)\big )~\textrm{d}x\right| \le C\Vert \lambda \Vert ^{-\delta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ie(λ1f1(x)+⋯+λkfk(x))dx≤C‖λ‖-δ.

The second half of Sect. 3 contains a proof of Theorem 1.7, which builds upon Theorem 3.1 but requires a more delicate analysis. Lastly, in Sect. 4 we prove Theorem 1.10 and discuss further potential extensions of Koksma’s theorem to the multidimensional setting.

A survey of metric uniform distribution

Given a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_n(x))_{n=1}^\infty $$\end{document}(un(x))n=1∞ depending on a parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, we are interested in conditions which guarantee that for almost every value of x the sequence is uniformly distributed. The first result in this direction, Theorem 1.4, was obtained by Weyl in the pioneering work [15], thereby starting the field of metric uniform distribution.

In this section, we present two fundamental theorems in this area, due to Weyl and Koksma, and present modern proofs. We pay particular attention to the quantitative aspects of the assumption in Weyl’s theorem and define the notion of scattered sequences, which slightly extends its scope. Throughout the section, we adapt the relatively simple proof of Theorem 1.4 (presented in subsection 2.1) to handle progressively more general results, culminating in subsection 2.7 with a proof of Theorem 1.6, a new multidimensional result that involves polynomial curves.

Metric uniform distribution – first results

We start this section with Theorem 2.1 which is a special case of Theorem 1.4 but already implies Borel’s theorem (Theorem 1.3). The proof of Theorem 2.1, albeit rather simple, provides a useful prototype for the proofs of stronger results obtained later in this paper.

Theorem 2.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_n)$$\end{document}(an) be an injective sequence of integers. Then, for (Lebesgue) almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_nx)$$\end{document}(anx) is uniformly distributed modulo 1.

Proof

In view of Weyl’s criterion (Theorem 1.2), which in the one-dimensional case states that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xn)n∈N is uniformly distributed mod 1 if and only if for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne(kx_n)$$\end{document}1N∑n=1Ne(kxn) tends to 0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞, it suffices to show that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N the sequence of functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(x):=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne(ka_nx)$$\end{document}FN(x):=1N∑n=1Ne(kanx) converges to 0 almost everywhere. Since each function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N$$\end{document}FN is periodic with period one, we may restrict its domain to [0, 1). Using the general fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^N u_n -\frac{1}{M}\sum _{n=1}^M u_n \right| \le 2(1-N/M)$$\end{document}1N∑n=1Nun-1M∑n=1Mun≤2(1-N/M) whenever N<MN<M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_n)$$\end{document}(un) is a sequence of complex numbers with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|u_n|=1$$\end{document}|un|=1, it is enough to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{N^2}(x)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}FN2(x)→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞ for almost every x∈ [0,1)x∈[0,1). This is obviously equivalent to the statement that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big |F_{N^2}(x)\big |^2\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}|FN2(x)|2→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞ for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R. As any summable sequence must tend to zero, we need only to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(x):=\sum _N|F_{N^2}(x)|^2<\infty $$\end{document}F(x):=∑N|FN2(x)|2<∞ for almost every x∈ [0,1)x∈[0,1). To establish this fact, it suffices to show that the integral \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^1F(x)~\textrm{d}x$$\end{document}∫01F(x)dx is finite. Using the monotone convergence theorem, we get2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^1F(x)~\textrm{d}x = \sum _{N=1}^\infty \int _0^1\big |F_{N^2}(x)\big |^2~\textrm{d}x = \sum _{N=1}^\infty \big \Vert F_{N^2}(x)\big \Vert ^2_{L^2[0,1]} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫01F(x)dx=∑N=1∞∫01|FN2(x)|2dx=∑N=1∞‖FN2(x)‖L2[0,1]2and, using orthogonality of characters (together with the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_n\ne a_m$$\end{document}an≠am whenever n≠ mn≠m),2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big \Vert F_{N}(x)\big \Vert ^2_{L^2[0,1]} = \frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\big \langle e(k a_n x),e(k a_mx)\big \rangle _{L^2[0,1]} = \frac{1}{N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FN(x)‖L2[0,1]2=1N2∑n,m=1N〈e(kanx),e(kamx)〉L2[0,1]=1N.Equations (2.1) and (2.2) together show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^1F(x)~\textrm{d}x= \sum _{N=1}^\infty \big \Vert F_{N^2}(x)\big \Vert ^2_{L^2[0,1]}= \sum _{N=1}^\infty 1/N^2<\infty $$\end{document}∫01F(x)dx=∑N=1∞‖FN2(x)‖L2[0,1]2=∑N=1∞1/N2<∞, finishing the proof. □ □

Theorem 2.1 also holds for sufficiently spread-out sequences of real numbers. Before addressing the full version of this extension in Theorem 2.11, we discuss first a special case that suffices to recover Theorem 1.4 stated in the introduction.

Theorem 2.2

(Weyl, 1916) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_n)$$\end{document}(an) be a sequence of real numbers and suppose that there exists a gap g>0g>0 satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|a_n-a_m|\ge g$$\end{document}|an-am|≥g for all n≠ mn≠m. Then, for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_nx)$$\end{document}(anx) is uniformly distributed modulo 1.

The proof of Theorem 2.2 requires only minor modifications to the proof of Theorem 2.1 presented. When the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_n$$\end{document}an are not integers, the functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\mapsto e(k a_n x)$$\end{document}x↦e(kanx) are not characters on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}/\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}R/Z and hence are not pairwise orthogonal, invalidating the computation in (2.2). Nevertheless, it turns out that when a-ba-b is sufficiently large, the functions x↦ e(k a x)x↦e(kax) and x↦ e(k b x)x↦e(kbx) are “almost orthogonal.” This asymptotic orthogonality allows us to estimate the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne(k a_n x)$$\end{document}FN(x)=1N∑n=1Ne(kanx) and is formalized using the following inequality1 due to van der Corput [13]. (We use here the version from [7, Lemma 2.1, page 15].)

Lemma 2.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:[a,b]\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:[a,b]→R be differentiable and such that f'f′ is continuous and monotone. Then,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \int _a^be\big (f(x)\big )~\textrm{d}x\right| \le \max \left( \frac{1}{|f'(a)|},\frac{1}{|f'(b)|}\right) .$$\end{document}∫abe(f(x))dx≤max1|f′(a)|,1|f′(b)|.

Using Lemma 2.3, it follows that22.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big |\big \langle e(k a_n \cdot ),e(k a_m\cdot )\big \rangle _{L^2}\big |=\left| \int e( k (a_n-a_m)x)~\textrm{d}x\right| \ll \frac{1}{|a_n-a_m|}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|〈e(kan·),e(kam·)〉L2|=∫e(k(an-am)x)dx≪1|an-am|.Since the set of real numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:=\{a_n:n\in \mathbb {N}\}$$\end{document}A:={an:n∈N} has any pair of points at distance at least g>0g>0, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{n=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{|a_n-a_m|}\le \sum _{n=1}^{m-1}\frac{2}{g n}\ll \log m$$\end{document}∑n=1m-11|an-am|≤∑n=1m-12gn≪logm and hence2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{1\le n<m\le N}\frac{1}{|a_n-a_m|}\ll N\log N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑1≤n<m≤N1|an-am|≪NlogN.Combining (2.3) and (2.4), it follows that the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(x):=\sum _N|F_{N^2}(x)|^2$$\end{document}F(x):=∑N|FN2(x)|2 satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _a^bF(x)~\textrm{d}x= \sum _{N=1}^\infty \big \Vert F_{N^2}(x)\big \Vert ^2_{L^2[a,b]}\ll \sum _{N=1}^\infty (\log N/N)^2<\infty $$\end{document}∫abF(x)dx=∑N=1∞‖FN2(x)‖L2[a,b]2≪∑N=1∞(logN/N)2<∞, finishing the proof of Theorem 2.2.

A far reaching extension of Theorem 2.2 was obtained by Koksma in [6, Satz 3] (see [7, Theorem 4.3, page 34]).

Theorem 2.4

(Koksma) Let a<ba<b, and for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_n\in C^1([a,b],\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}un∈C1([a,b],R). Assume that there exists g>0g>0 such that whenever n≠ mn≠m the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\mapsto \big |u'_n(x)-u_m'(x)\big |$$\end{document}x↦|un′(x)-um′(x)| is monotone and bounded from below by g.

Then for almost every x∈ [a,b]x∈[a,b] the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (u_n(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(un(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed mod 1.

Note that Theorem 2.4 implies Theorem 2.2 when applied to linear functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_n(x)=a_nx$$\end{document}un(x)=anx. Another famous corollary of Theorem 2.4 is that the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xn)n∈N is uniformly distributed for almost every x>1x>1, which can be derived from Theorem 2.4 by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_n(x)=x^n$$\end{document}un(x)=xn.

One can prove Theorem 2.4 by following the same basic steps as the proof of Theorem 2.2 outlined. In order to obtain an appropriate analogue of (2.2), one utilizes Lemma 2.3. More precisely, denoting by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N:[a,b]\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}FN:[a,b]→R the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne(ku_n(x))$$\end{document}FN(x)=1N∑n=1Ne(kun(x)), it follows from Lemma 2.3 that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_N\Vert ^2_{L^2[a,b]}\le & \frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\max \left( \frac{1}{|u_n'(a)-u_m'(a)|},\frac{1}{|u_n'(b)-u_m'(b)|}\right) \\ \le & \frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\frac{1}{|u_n'(a)-u_m'(a)|}+\frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\frac{1}{|u_n'(b)-u_m'(b)|}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2[a,b]2≤1N2∑n,m=1Nmax1|un′(a)-um′(a)|,1|un′(b)-um′(b)|≤1N2∑n,m=1N1|un′(a)-um′(a)|+1N2∑n,m=1N1|un′(b)-um′(b)|.Since both sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u_n'(a):n\in \mathbb {N}\}$$\end{document}{un′(a):n∈N} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u_n'(b):n\in \mathbb {N}\}$$\end{document}{un′(b):n∈N} are g-separated sets of real numbers, it follows (using the same reasoning as in obtaining (2.4)) that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert F_N\Vert ^2_{L^2(I)}\ll \log {N}/N$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2≪logN/N. The conclusion of the proof now is the same as for Theorem 2.2. By sharpening various elements of the above proof, one can obtain the more general Theorem 2.20 which is formulated and proved in Sect. 2.5.

A more general view of Theorem 2.1

An equivalent form of the definition of uniform distribution for a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(xn)n∈N of real numbers is that for every continuous function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C(\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}f∈C(T),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Nf(\{x_n\})=\int _0^1 f(x)~\textrm{d}x. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞1N∑n=1Nf({xn})=∫01f(x)dx.A similar characterization of uniform distribution for sequences in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd can be formulated in the obvious way. (These equivalent forms were already present in [15].) In light of this fact, we can rephrase Theorem 2.1 as follows:2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \forall f\in C(\mathbb {T})\qquad \frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Nf(\{a_nx\})\rightarrow \int f(x)~\textrm{d}x \quad a.s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∀f∈C(T)1N∑n=1Nf({anx})→∫f(x)dxa.s.While (2.5) resembles an ergodic theorem, a closer look at the proof of Theorem 2.1 given above reveals that the same statement holds when the averaging sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\dots ,N\}$$\end{document}{1,⋯,N} get replaced by any (not necessarily Følner) sequence of strictly nested finite sets in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}$$\end{document}N, leading to the following ostensibly stronger result.

Theorem 2.5
