Real analytic versus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\infty $$\end{document}C∞ functions

Having established the relevance of scattered sequences, in this subsection we discuss smoothness constraints on a function f in order for it to satisfy the following property.2.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {For every scattered sequence }a:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R},\text { the sequence }\big (a(n)f(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}\text { is u.d. a.e.} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}For every scattered sequencea:N→R,the sequence(a(n)f(x))n∈Nis u.d. a.e.Theorem 2.11 states that the identity function f:x↦ xf:x↦x satisfies (2.10), and an immediate corollary is that the same is true whenever the pushforward measure4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_*\mu $$\end{document}f∗μ is absolutely continuous with respect to μ μ. In particular, this is true whenever f is a non-constant (real) analytic function:

Proposition 2.24

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:R→R be non-constant and real analytic. Then, the pushforward measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_*\mu $$\end{document}f∗μ is absolutely continuous with respect to μ μ and hence f satisfies (2.10).

Proof

Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}A⊂R has μ (A)=0μ(A)=0; we need to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (f^{-1}(A))=0$$\end{document}μ(f-1(A))=0. Since f is analytic and non-constant, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R is the union of the countable set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x:f'(x)=0\}$$\end{document}{x:f′(x)=0} and countable many compact intervals where f'f′ is nonzero. It suffices to prove that for any such interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (I\cap f^{-1}(A))=0$$\end{document}μ(I∩f-1(A))=0.

Let J=f(I)J=f(I). Since f:I→ Jf:I→J is a bijection, it has an inverse g:J→ Ig:J→I. The change of variable formula now implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu (I\cap f^{-1}(A))= & \int _I 1_{f(x)\in A}~\textrm{d}x = \int _J1_{u\in A}~\textrm{d}u/|f'(g(u))| \\ \le & \max _{x\in I}\frac{1}{|f'(x)|}\int _J1_{u\in A}~\textrm{d}u = \max _{x\in I}\frac{1}{|f'(x)|}\mu (J\cap A)=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ(I∩f-1(A))=∫I1f(x)∈Adx=∫J1u∈Adu/|f′(g(u))|≤maxx∈I1|f′(x)|∫J1u∈Adu=maxx∈I1|f′(x)|μ(J∩A)=0.□ □

Observe that if f is a constant function, then it does not satisfy (2.10). More generally, if there exists a level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-1}(\{y\}):=\{x\in \mathbb {R}:f(x)=y\}$$\end{document}f-1({y}):={x∈R:f(x)=y} with positive measure for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}y∈R, then choosing a to be any injective sequence taking values in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{m\in \mathbb {N}:\Vert my\Vert _\mathbb {T}<1/3\}$$\end{document}{m∈N:‖my‖T<1/3} we see that there is a positive measure set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R for which the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a(n)f(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a(n)f(x))n∈N is not uniformly distributed mod 1. One might hope that this is the only obstruction, and any smooth function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:R→R whose level sets have measure zero satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_*\mu \ll \mu $$\end{document}f∗μ≪μ and hence f satisfies (2.10). However, this is not the case: An example by Yuval Peres answering a question of the authors on Math StackExchange [4] produces a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈C∞(R) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (f^{-1}(y))=0$$\end{document}μ(f-1(y))=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}y∈R and such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (f^{-1}(\Lambda ))>0$$\end{document}μ(f-1(Λ))>0 where Λ Λ is a Cantor set on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (\Lambda )=0$$\end{document}μ(Λ)=0.

In view of Peres’ example and Proposition 2.24, in this paper all functions are assumed to be real analytic.

A multidimensional Weyl theorem along polynomial curves

We conclude this section by demonstrating how a small variation of Weyl’s proof already suffices to establish Theorem 1.6 which involves polynomial curves and uniform distribution in the multidimensional setting.

The presence of the scalar product in Weyl’s criterion for multidimensional sequences (Theorem 1.2) leads us to consider the following notion for k-tuples of sequences.

Definition 2.25

(Jointly scattered sequences) Given sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R, we say that they are jointly scattered if for every nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=(v_1,\dots ,v_k)\in \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}v=(v1,⋯,vk)∈Rk the linear combination \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1a_1+\cdots +v_ka_k$$\end{document}v1a1+⋯+vkak is scattered.

For the proof of Theorem 1.6, and its extension Theorem 1.10, we will require an extension of van der Corput’s Lemma 2.3 involving higher derivatives.

Theorem 2.26

([7, Lemma 2.2, page 16],[12, Proposition 2, Chapter VIII]) Let a<ba<b be real, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}d∈N, d≥ 2d≥2 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^{d}[a,b]$$\end{document}f∈Cd[a,b] be real valued. Then,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| \int _a^be\big (f(x)\big )~\textrm{d}x\right| \le C_d\sup \Big \{\big |f^{(d)}(x)\big |^{-1/d}:x\in [a,b]\Big \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫abe(f(x))dx≤Cdsup{|f(d)(x)|-1/d:x∈[a,b]},where the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_d$$\end{document}Cd depends only on d.

Strictly speaking, Lemma 2.26 does not contain Lemma 2.3 as a special case, since it requires d≥ 2d≥2. In the d=1d=1 case, one needs the additional assumption that f'f′ is monotone; this assumption is unnecessary in Lemma 2.26 because the inequality holds trivially if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{(d)}$$\end{document}f(d) has a zero, and otherwise, there is a bounded number of sub-intervals of [a, b] where f'f′ is monotone.

We are now ready to prove Theorem 1.6 which we restate here for the convenience of the reader.

Theorem

1.6 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\cdots , p_k\in \mathbb {R}[x]$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk∈R[x] be non-constant polynomials, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R be jointly scattered. Then for Lebesgue-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)p_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)p_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)p1(x),⋯,ak(n)pk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\dots ,p_k\in \mathbb {R}[x]$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk∈R[x] be non-constant polynomials, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R be jointly scattered sequences. Our goal is to show that for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)p_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)p_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)p1(x),⋯,ak(n)pk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Using Theorem 1.2, if suffices to show that for each fixed nonzero vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=(v_1,\dots ,v_k)\in \mathbb {Z}^k$$\end{document}v=(v1,⋯,vk)∈Zk the sequence of functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}FN:R→C defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_N(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\big (v_1a_1(n)p_1(x)+\cdots +v_ka_k(n)p_k(x)\big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FN(x)=1N∑n=1Ne(v1a1(n)p1(x)+⋯+vkak(n)pk(x))tend to zero almost everywhere on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R is the countable union of (not necessarily disjoint) compact intervals, it suffices to establish almost everywhere convergence to 0 on each such interval.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}d∈N be the maximal degree among the polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{p_i:v_i\ne 0\}$$\end{document}{pi:vi≠0}. Since v is nonzero, d is well defined. For a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _n:x\mapsto v_1a_1(n)p_1(x)+\cdots +v_ka_k(n)p_k(x)$$\end{document}ϕn:x↦v1a1(n)p1(x)+⋯+vkak(n)pk(x) is a polynomial of degree at most d, and the coefficient b(n) of the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^d$$\end{document}xd in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _n$$\end{document}ϕn can be expressed as a nonzero linear combination of the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1(n),\dots ,a_k(n)$$\end{document}a1(n),⋯,ak(n). Since these sequences are jointly scattered, the sequence b(n) is scattered. The d-th derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _n$$\end{document}ϕn is the constant function d!b(n), and in particular it is also a scattered sequence.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R be an arbitrary compact interval. To show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}FN→0 almost everywhere on I, we consider the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big \Vert F_N\big \Vert _{L^2(I)}^2 = \int _I\left| \frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\big (\phi _n(x)\big )\right| ^2~\textrm{d}x = \frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\int _I e\Big (\phi _n(x)-\phi _m(x)\Big )~\textrm{d}x.$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2=∫I1N∑n=1Ne(ϕn(x))2dx=1N2∑n,m=1N∫Ie(ϕn(x)-ϕm(x))dx.Using Lemma 2.26, and (2.6) for the sequence b(n), we can estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big \Vert F_N\big \Vert _{L^2(I)}^2 \le C_d\frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\min \Big (\big |b(n)-b(m)\big |^{-1/d},|I|\Big )\le \frac{1}{(\log N)^{1+\epsilon }}$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2≤Cd1N2∑n,m=1Nmin(|b(n)-b(m)|-1/d,|I|)≤1(logN)1+ϵfor some ε >0ϵ>0 and every sufficiently large N (depending only on d and |I|).

To finish the proof, we use the same argument as in the proof of Theorem 2.20. Let ε '>0ϵ′>0 be sufficiently small so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\epsilon ')(1+\epsilon )>1$$\end{document}(1-ϵ′)(1+ϵ)>1. Setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_r:=e^{r^{1-\epsilon '}}$$\end{document}Nr:=e1-ϵ′, we deduce that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{r=1}^\infty \Vert F_{N_r}\Vert ^2_{L^2(I)} \le \sum _{r=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(\log N_r)^{1+\epsilon }} = \sum _{r=1}^\infty \frac{1}{r^{(1-\epsilon ')(1+\epsilon )}} < \infty ,$$\end{document}∑r=1∞‖FNr‖L2(I)2≤∑r=1∞1(logNr)1+ϵ=∑r=1∞1r(1-ϵ′)(1+ϵ)<∞,which implies that the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\mapsto \sum _{r=1}^\infty \big |F_{N_r}(x)\big |^2$$\end{document}x↦∑r=1∞|FNr(x)|2 has a finite integral and hence is finite for almost every x∈ Ix∈I. In particular, for almost every x∈ Ix∈I the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{N_r}(x)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}FNr(x)→0. The conclusion now follows from Lemma 2.23. □ □

Remark 2.27

The proof of Theorem 1.6 can be adapted to handle the case where the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are scattered (but not necessarily jointly scattered) and the polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\dots ,p_k$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk have distinct degrees. However, to prove Theorem 1.5 in full generality, we need a different method. To clarify this point, consider the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big ((n+\log n)x^2,n(x^2+x)\big )$$\end{document}((n+logn)x2,n(x2+x)), corresponding to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a_1(n)=n+\log n,\qquad a_2(n)=n,\qquad p_1(x)=x^2,\qquad p_2(x)=x^2+x. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a1(n)=n+logn,a2(n)=n,p1(x)=x2,p2(x)=x2+x.When v=(1,-1)v=(1,-1), in the proof above, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _n(x)=(\log n)x^2-nx$$\end{document}ϕn(x)=(logn)x2-nx. Although this sequence is uniformly distributed mod 1 for almost all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, this cannot be ascertained by differentiating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _n$$\end{document}ϕn twice, because the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\log n)x^2$$\end{document}(logn)x2, which has the same second derivative, is not uniformly distributed for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R.

In the next section, we present a proof of Theorem 3.1 which contains Theorem 1.5 as a special case.

Multidimensional Weyl theorem along analytic curves

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 1.7.

The case of linearly independent functions

First we deal with the case of Theorem 1.7 which involves linearly independent functions. In this case, we obtain the following “if and only if” statement which is of interest on its own.

Theorem 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be analytic functions. Then, the following are equivalent: The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} is linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.For every scattered sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R, there exists a full measure set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R for which the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} is linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.

The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} is linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.

For every scattered sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R, there exists a full measure set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R for which the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

For every scattered sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R, there exists a full measure set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R for which the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Proof

First we prove the implication (2)⇒ ⇒(1). Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_1,\dots ,c_k\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c1,⋯,ck∈R are not all zero but the linear combination \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_1f_1+\cdots +c_kf_k$$\end{document}c1f1+⋯+ckfk is a constant function with value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}α∈R. Then, taking\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_i(n)={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} n& \text { if }c_i=0\\ c_in& \text { if }c_i\ne 0\text { and }\alpha =0\\ \tfrac{c_i}{\alpha }n& \text { if }c_i\ne 0\text { and }\alpha \ne 0, \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}ai(n)=nifci=0cinifci≠0andα=0ciαnifci≠0andα≠0,which are all scattered sequences, we claim that the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is not uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R.

To verify the claim, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_i=1_{c_i\ne 0}$$\end{document}vi=1ci≠0 for each i=1,… ,ki=1,⋯,k and note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1a_1(n)f_1(x)+\cdots +v_ka_k(n)f_k(x)={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0& \text { if }\alpha =0\\ n& \text {otherwise}, \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}v1a1(n)f1(x)+⋯+vkak(n)fk(x)=0ifα=0notherwise,for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N. It follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\Big (v_1a_1(n)f_1(x)+\cdots +v_ka_k(n)f_k(x)\Big )=1\ne 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1N∑n=1Ne(v1a1(n)f1(x)+⋯+vkak(n)fk(x))=1≠0,so the Weyl criterion (Theorem 1.2) implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is not uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk, verifying the claim and finishing the proof of the implication (2)⇒ ⇒(1).

Next we prove the converse implication (1)⇒ ⇒(2). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be analytic functions such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} are linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R and fix scattered sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak. By the Weyl criterion, for any fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x))_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is u.d. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk if and only if for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=(v_1,\dots ,v_k)\in \mathbb {Z}^k\setminus \{\textbf{0}\}$$\end{document}v=(v1,⋯,vk)∈Zk\{0},3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\big (v_1a_1(n)f_1(x)+\cdots +v_ka_k(n)f_k(x)\big )=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞1N∑n=1Ne(v1a1(n)f1(x)+⋯+vkak(n)fk(x))=0.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}^k$$\end{document}Zk is countable, it suffices to show that for any given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathbb {Z}^k\setminus \{\textbf{0}\}$$\end{document}v∈Zk\{0}, (3.1) holds for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R. Fix from now on such v, and denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _n(x)=v_1a_1(n)f_1(x)+\cdots +v_ka_k(n)f_k(x)$$\end{document}ϕn(x)=v1a1(n)f1(x)+⋯+vkak(n)fk(x).

We seek to prove that the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\big (\phi _n(x)\big )$$\end{document}1N∑n=1Ne(ϕn(x)) converges to 0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞ almost everywhere in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R is a countable union of (not necessarily disjoint) compact intervals, it suffices to show that for each fixed compact interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R, the functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N:I\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}FN:I→C given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\big (\phi _n(x)\big )$$\end{document}FN(x)=1N∑n=1Ne(ϕn(x)) converge to 0 almost everywhere as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞. We fix such an interval I from now on.

We claim that there exists ε >0ϵ>0 such that3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_N\Vert _{L^2(I)}^2\ll \frac{1}{\log (N)^{1+\epsilon }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2≪1log(N)1+ϵ.Assuming for now that the claim is true, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}r∈N let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_r\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}Nr∈N be the smallest integer satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log N_r>r^{1-\epsilon /2}$$\end{document}logNr>r1-ϵ/2. It follows from (3.2) and the monotone convergence theorem that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(x):=\sum _{r=1}^\infty |F_{N_r}(x)|^2$$\end{document}S(x):=∑r=1∞|FNr(x)|2 has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert S\Vert _{L^1(I)}<\infty $$\end{document}‖S‖L1(I)<∞ and hence S(x)<∞ S(x)<∞ for almost every x∈ Ix∈I. This implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{N_r}(x)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}FNr(x)→0 for almost every x∈ Ix∈I, and in view of Lemma 2.23, it then implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(x)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}FN(x)→0 for almost every x∈ Ix∈I. This is what we wanted to show, so we are left to establish the claim (3.2).

Using Theorem 1.14, let C,δ >0C,δ>0 be such that (1.3) holds. For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n,m\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n,m∈N let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{n,m}\in \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}λn,m∈Rk be the vector\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{n,m}=\Big (v_1\big (a_1(n)-a_1(m)\big ),\dots ,v_k\big (a_k(n)-a_k(m)\big )\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λn,m=(v1(a1(n)-a1(m)),⋯,vk(ak(n)-ak(m))).Since v is nonzero, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\in \{1,\dots ,k\}$$\end{document}j∈{1,⋯,k} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_j\ne 0$$\end{document}vj≠0. It follows that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n,m\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n,m∈N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \lambda _{n,m}\Vert \ge |v_j|\cdot \big |a_j(n)-a_j(m)\big |$$\end{document}‖λn,m‖≥|vj|·|aj(n)-aj(m)| and hence3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \lambda _{n,m}\Vert ^{-\delta }\le |v_j|^{-\delta }\cdot \big |a_j(n)-a_j(m)\big |^{-\delta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖λn,m‖-δ≤|vj|-δ·|aj(n)-aj(m)|-δ.We rewrite the left-hand side of (3.2) as3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_N\Vert ^2_{L^2(I)} = \frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{1\le n,m\le N}\int _Ie\left( \sum _{i=1}^k\lambda _{n,m;i}f_i(x)\right) ~\textrm{d}x. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2=1N2∑1≤n,m≤N∫Ie∑i=1kλn,m;ifi(x)dx.For each n, m, the integrals in the right-hand side of (3.4) are bounded by |I|, so combining (1.3) with (3.3) and then using the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_j$$\end{document}aj is scattered via (2.6), we conclude that there exists ε >0ϵ>0 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert F_N\Vert $$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2≤C|vj|-δ1N2∑1≤n,m≤Nmin(|I|,|aj(n)-aj(m)|-δ)≪1(logN)1+ϵestablishing (3.2) and finishing the proof. □ □

The case of jointly scattered sequences

In this subsection, we prove the case of Theorem 1.7 where no independence assumption on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i$$\end{document}fi is made, but we require the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak to be jointly scattered; this is the content of the following theorem.

Theorem 3.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be non-constant analytic functions. Then, whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R are jointly scattered, for Lebesgue-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))n∈N is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Proof
