Multidimensional extensions of Koksma’s theorem

In this section, we explore multidimensional extensions of Koksma’s Theorem 2.4. In subsection 4.1, we present a proof of Theorem 1.10, which combines Theorem 2.4 with the multidimensional Theorems 1.5 and 1.6. Then, in subsection 4.2 we present some observations and conjectures that are motivated by Theorem 1.7.

Proof of Theorem 1.10

In this subsection, we prove Theorem 1.10 whose statement we now restate for convenience of the reader.

Theorem

1.10 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow (1,\infty )$$\end{document}g:R→(1,∞) be a non-constant analytic function and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}b:N→R tend to ∞ ∞ and be scattered. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1,\cdots , p_k\in \mathbb {R}[x]$$\end{document}p1,⋯,pk∈R[x] be non-constant polynomials, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak:N→R be scattered. Assume that either The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,orThe polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} are linearly independent.Then, for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (g(x)^{b(n)},a_1(n)p_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)p_k(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g(x)b(n),a1(n)p1(x),⋯,ak(n)pk(x)) is u.d. in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^{k+1}$$\end{document}Tk+1.

The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,or

The sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered,

or

The polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} are linearly independent.

The polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,p_1,\dots ,p_k\}$$\end{document}{1,p1,⋯,pk} are linearly independent.

Proof

In view of Weyl’s criterion (Theorem 1.2), it suffices to show that for any nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathbb {Z}^{k+1}$$\end{document}v∈Zk+1, the sequence of functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}FN:R→C given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_N(x):=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\left( v_0g(x)^{b(n)}+\sum _{i=1}^kv_ia_i(n)p_i(x)\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FN(x):=1N∑n=1Nev0g(x)b(n)+∑i=1kviai(n)pi(x)tends to zero almost everywhere. Since g is analytic, the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x\in \mathbb {R}:g'(x)=0\}$$\end{document}{x∈R:g′(x)=0} is countable and closed. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R can be covered, up to a countable set, by countably many compact intervals where g'(x)g′(x) does not vanish. Hence we may restrict attention to a single compact interval I where g'(x)g′(x) does not vanish, and our goal is to show that the (restriction to I of the) sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(x)$$\end{document}FN(x) tends to zero almost everywhere on I.

Whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_0=0$$\end{document}v0=0, the conclusion that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(x)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}FN(x)→0 for a.e. x∈ Ix∈I follows from the exact same argument that was used to prove Theorem 1.7. Suppose next that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_0\ne 0$$\end{document}v0≠0.

We begin by computing the norms\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_N\Vert ^2_{L^2(I)}=\frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\int _Ie\left( v_0\big (g(x)^{b(n)}-g(x)^{b(m)}\big )+\sum _{i=1}^kv_ip_i(x)\big (a_i(n)-a_i(m)\big )\right) ~\textrm{d}x \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2=1N2∑n,m=1N∫Iev0(g(x)b(n)-g(x)b(m))+∑i=1kvipi(x)(ai(n)-ai(m))dxWe choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}d∈N larger than the degree of all the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i$$\end{document}pi and use Lemma 2.26 to deduce that4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_N\Vert ^2_{L^2(I)} \le \frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N \min \bigg (C_d\cdot \sup \Big \{\Big |f_{n,m}^{(d)}(x)\Big |^{-1/d}:x\in I\Big \},1\bigg ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2≤1N2∑n,m=1Nmin(Cd·sup{|fn,m(d)(x)|-1/d:x∈I},1),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{n,m}(x)=v_0\big (g(x)^{b(n)}-g(x)^{b(m)}\big )$$\end{document}fn,m(x)=v0(g(x)b(n)-g(x)b(m)). Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (x,b)=g(x)^b$$\end{document}ψ(x,b)=g(x)b for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,b\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x,b∈R, and fix a bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0>0$$\end{document}b0>0 to be determined later. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n,m\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n,m∈N such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(n),b(m)>b_0$$\end{document}b(n),b(m)>b0, using the mean value theorem we can estimate4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \forall x\in I,\qquad \Big |f_{n,m}^{(d)}(x)\Big | = |v_0|\cdot \left| \frac{\partial ^d\psi }{\partial x^d}\big (x,b(n)\big )-\frac{\partial ^d\psi }{\partial x^d}\big (x,b(m)\big )\right| \ge C\big |b(n)-b(m)\big |, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∀x∈I,|fn,m(d)(x)|=|v0|·∂dψ∂xd(x,b(n))-∂dψ∂xd(x,b(m))≥C|b(n)-b(m)|,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=C(b_0)=|v_0|\cdot \inf \left\{ \left| \frac{\partial }{\partial b}\frac{\partial ^d\psi }{\partial x^d}(x,b)\right| :x\in I, |b|>b_0\right\} $$\end{document}C=C(b0)=|v0|·inf∂∂b∂dψ∂xd(x,b):x∈I,|b|>b0.

We now show that for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0>0$$\end{document}b0>0, the value of C is positive. A routine induction (or direct computation) shows that for each fixed x∈ Ix∈I and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}d∈N, the map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi _x:b\mapsto \frac{1}{\psi (x,b)}\cdot \frac{\partial ^d\psi }{\partial x^d}(x,b) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕx:b↦1ψ(x,b)·∂dψ∂xd(x,b)is a polynomial of degree d. Moreover, the coefficients of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _x$$\end{document}ϕx depend continuously on x (in fact, they are polynomials on g(x) and its derivatives) and the leading coefficient is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g'(x))^d$$\end{document}(g′(x))d. We further compute\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\partial }{\partial b}\frac{\partial ^d\psi }{\partial x^d}(x,b) = \frac{\partial }{\partial b}\big ( g(x)^b\cdot \phi _x(b)\big ) = g(x)^b\Big (\phi '_x(b)-\phi _x(b)\log \big (g(x)\big )\Big ).$$\end{document}∂∂b∂dψ∂xd(x,b)=∂∂b(g(x)b·ϕx(b))=g(x)b(ϕx′(b)-ϕx(b)log(g(x))).The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_x:b\mapsto \phi '_x(b)-\phi _x(b)\log \big (g(x)\big )$$\end{document}Px:b↦ϕx′(b)-ϕx(b)log(g(x)) is again a polynomial whose coefficients depend continuously on x and whose leading coefficient is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g'(x))^d\log \big (g(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g′(x))dlog(g(x)). Since I is compact, g'(x)≠ 0g′(x)≠0 on I and g takes values in (1,∞ )(1,∞), it follows that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0$$\end{document}b0 is sufficiently large (depending on I, g and d), then whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|b|>b_0$$\end{document}|b|>b0 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_x(b)>1$$\end{document}Px(b)>1 for every x∈ Ix∈I. For this value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0$$\end{document}b0, the value of C in (4.2) is positive.

Combining the two estimates (4.1) and (4.2) with the fact that b is 1/d-scattered, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_N\Vert ^2_{L^2(I)}\ll & \frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{n,m=1}^N\min \big (|b(n)-b(m)|^{-1/d},1\big )+o(1) \\ \ll & \frac{1}{(\log N)^{1+\epsilon '}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖FN‖L2(I)2≪1N2∑n,m=1Nmin(|b(n)-b(m)|-1/d,1)+o(1)≪1(logN)1+ϵ′for some ε '>0ϵ′>0. To finish the proof, we use yet again the same argument as in the proof of Theorem 2.20. Let ε ''>0ϵ′′>0 be sufficiently small so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\epsilon '')(1+\epsilon ')>1$$\end{document}(1-ϵ′′)(1+ϵ′)>1. Setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_r:=e^{r^{1-\epsilon ''}}$$\end{document}Nr:=e1-ϵ′′, we deduce that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{r=1}^\infty \Vert F_{N_r}\Vert ^2_{L^2(I)} \le \sum _{r=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(\log N_r)^{1+\epsilon '}} = \sum _{r=1}^\infty \frac{1}{r^{(1-\epsilon '')(1+\epsilon ')}} < \infty ,$$\end{document}∑r=1∞‖FNr‖L2(I)2≤∑r=1∞1(logNr)1+ϵ′=∑r=1∞1r(1-ϵ′′)(1+ϵ′)<∞,which implies that the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\mapsto \sum _{r=1}^\infty \big |F_{N_r}(x)\big |^2$$\end{document}x↦∑r=1∞|FNr(x)|2 has a finite integral and hence is finite for almost every x∈ Ix∈I. In particular, for almost every x∈ Ix∈I the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{N_r}(x)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}FNr(x)→0. The conclusion now follows from Lemma 2.23. □ □

Note that the proof uses crucially the fact that only the first term was not a polynomial of bounded degree in x, so when applying enough derivatives all terms vanish except for the first one. The same method faces more obstacles when trying to prove that, for example, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^n,x^{2n})$$\end{document}(xn,x2n) is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 for almost every x>1x>1; we are unable to either prove or disprove this statement (see Conjecture 4.3 for a more general statement). In short, in such cases we cannot rule out the possibility that the supremum in (4.1) is large, or even infinite, for many choices of n, m.

Multidimensional variants of Theorem 2.20

While Theorem 1.9 gives a satisfactory extension of Weyl’s theorem to curves in higher dimensions, similar multidimensional extensions of Koksma’s Theorem 2.20 are significantly more challenging. Theorem 1.10 proved in the previous subsection, while containing the one-dimensional Theorem 2.20 as a special case, is suggestive of several further amplifications. The goal of this subsection is to discuss some conjectures in this direction.

We start the discussion with the following simple proposition, which for expository reasons we formulate only in two dimensions.

Proposition 4.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(un)n∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(v_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(vn)n∈N satisfy the conditions of Theorem 2.20 on the intervals I and J respectively. Then, there is a full measure set of pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y)\in I\times J$$\end{document}(x,y)∈I×J for which the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (u_n(x),v_n(y)\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(un(x),vn(y))n∈N is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2.

Proof

In view of Weyl’s criterion (Theorem 1.2), it suffices to show that for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a,b)\in \mathbb {Z}^2\setminus \{(0,0)\}$$\end{document}(a,b)∈Z2\{(0,0)} there is a full measure set of pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y)\in I\times J$$\end{document}(x,y)∈I×J for which4.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{N}\sum _{n=1}^Ne\big (au_n(x)+bv_n(y)\big )=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞1N∑n=1Ne(aun(x)+bvn(y))=0.If a≠ 0a≠0, then we will show that for every y∈ Jy∈J there is a full measure set of x∈ Ix∈I for which (4.3) holds. Indeed, in this case the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_n(x):=au_n(x)+bv_n(y)$$\end{document}wn(x):=aun(x)+bvn(y) satisfies the conditions of Theorem 2.20, which then implies that for almost every x∈ Ix∈I the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_n(x)$$\end{document}wn(x) is uniformly distributed modulo one. Weyl’s criterion then implies for each such x, (4.3) holds.

If a=0a=0, then b≠ 0b≠0. In this case, we can reverse the roles of the two coordinates above and deduce that for every x∈ Ix∈I there exists a full measure set of y∈ Jy∈J for which (4.3) holds. □ □

One may hope to find a “curve” version of the above proposition that extends Koksma’s Theorem 2.20 in the same way as Theorem 1.7 extends Weyl’s Theorem 2.2. However, one needs to heed the following simple (counter)example.

Example 4.2

Take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_n(x)=nx$$\end{document}un(x)=nx, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_n(x)=n\sqrt{x}$$\end{document}vn(x)=nx, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1(x)=x$$\end{document}f1(x)=x, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2(x)=x^2$$\end{document}f2(x)=x2. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1$$\end{document}u1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_2$$\end{document}u2 both satisfy the conditions of Theorem 2.20 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,f_2\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,f2} are linearly independent; however, the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_n(f_1(x)),v_n(f_2(x)))=(nx,nx)$$\end{document}(un(f1(x)),vn(f2(x)))=(nx,nx) is not uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 for any x.

We propose two possible ways to avoid the pitfall illustrated by Example 4.2.

Conjecture 4.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(un)n∈N satisfy the conditions of Theorem 2.20. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be analytic functions such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} are linearly independent. Then for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big (u_n(f_1(x)),\dots ,u_n(f_k(x))\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(un(f1(x)),⋯,un(fk(x)))n∈N.is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Conjecture 4.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R be a compact interval, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, and for each i=1,… ,ki=1,⋯,k, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_{i;n})_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(ui;n)n∈N be a sequence of functions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1(I,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}C1(I,R). Assume that the derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,n}'$$\end{document}ui,n′ are monotone for every i and n. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be analytic functions.

Assume that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a∈R the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{1,n}'(a),\dots ,u_{k,n}'(a)$$\end{document}u1,n′(a),⋯,uk,n′(a) are jointly scattered. Then, for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big (u_{1,n}(f_1(x)),\dots ,u_{k,n}(f_k(x))\big )_{n\in \mathbb {N}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(u1,n(f1(x)),⋯,uk,n(fk(x)))n∈N.is uniformly distributed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^k$$\end{document}Tk.

Note that either of the above conjectures implies that the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^n,x^{2n})_{n=1}^\infty $$\end{document}(xn,x2n)n=1∞ is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 for almost every x>1x>1.

Next we focus our attention on the special case when the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_n(x)$$\end{document}un(x) take either the form a(n)f(x) or the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(x)^{b(n)}$$\end{document}g(x)b(n). This was the setting of Question 1.13. We now conjecture a possible answer, which extends the scope of Conjecture 1.11 formulated in the introduction.

Conjecture 4.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1,\dots ,g_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow (1,\infty )$$\end{document}g1,⋯,gk:R→(1,∞) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1,\dots ,f_k:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f1,⋯,fk:R→R be analytic functions. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k,b_1,\dots ,b_k:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak,b1,⋯,bk:N→R be sequences such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1,\dots ,a_k$$\end{document}a1,⋯,ak are jointly scattered and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_1,\dots ,b_k$$\end{document}b1,⋯,bk are jointly scattered.

Assume that both sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,g_1,\dots ,g_k\}$$\end{document}{1,g1,⋯,gk} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,f_1,\dots ,f_k\}$$\end{document}{1,f1,⋯,fk} are linearly independent over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R. Then for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big (g_1(x)^{b_1(n)},\dots ,g_k(x)^{b_k(n)},a_1(n)f_1(x),\dots ,a_k(n)f_k(x)\big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(g1(x)b1(n),⋯,gk(x)bk(n),a1(n)f1(x),⋯,ak(n)fk(x))is uniformly distributed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^{2k}$$\end{document}T2k.

Using a “change of variable trick” we can prove the following result, corresponding to a special case of Conjecture 4.5. Note that, in comparison with Theorem 1.10, we have only one f, but it can be any analytic function, not necessarily a polynomial.

Corollary

1.12 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f,g:\mathbb {R}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f,g:R→R be non-constant analytic functions such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\mathbb {R})\subset (1,\infty )$$\end{document}g(R)⊂(1,∞) and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b:\mathbb {N}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a,b:N→R be scattered sequences. Then for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x∈R the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (g(x)^{b(n)},a(n)f(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g(x)b(n),a(n)f(x)) is u.d. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_0\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I0⊂R be a compact interval where f'f′ does not vanish and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I:=f(I_0)$$\end{document}I:=f(I0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=g\circ f^{-1}$$\end{document}h=g∘f-1. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:I_0\rightarrow I$$\end{document}f:I0→I is a bijection, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-1}$$\end{document}f-1 and hence h is well defined on I. Applying Theorem 1.10, we conclude that for almost every x∈ Ix∈I, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (h(x)^{b(n)},a(n)x\big )$$\end{document}(h(x)b(n),a(n)x) is u.d. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2. In view of Proposition 2.24, it follows that for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in I_0$$\end{document}x∈I0, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (g(x)^{b(n)},a(n)f(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g(x)b(n),a(n)f(x)) is u.d. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2.

Since f is analytic and non-constant, the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x:f'(x)=0\}$$\end{document}{x:f′(x)=0} is discrete, and hence, it has zero measure and its complement can be covered with countably many compact intervals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_0$$\end{document}I0. The previous paragraph shows for each such interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_0$$\end{document}I0, for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in I_0$$\end{document}x∈I0, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (g(x)^{b(n)},a(n)f(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g(x)b(n),a(n)f(x)) is u.d. on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2, and the conclusion follows from the fact that the countable union of sets of measure zero has measure zero. □ □

Alas, the “change of variable” trick used in the proof of Theorem 1.12 does not extend to longer patterns such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (g(x)^{b(n)},a_1(n)f_1(x),a_2(n)f_2(x)\big )$$\end{document}(g(x)b(n),a1(n)f1(x),a2(n)f2(x)) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1$$\end{document}f1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2$$\end{document}f2 are arbitrary non-constant analytic functions.
